I know that probably sounds bad… but yes, I guess I do eat pretty quickly then.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you can stop eating once you've started on those late nights?\n\nPatient: I… yeah, I think that’s probably the hardest part to admit. I can tell myself I’m going to stop, and part of me really does want to, but once I’ve started there’s this point where I’m kind of watching myself go back to the food, or open another package, and I’m thinking, “Okay, that’s enough,” but it doesn’t quite feel like I’m the one deciding anymore. Like the decision already slipped away a little. Does that make sense?\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're in control of what you're eating during those late-night episodes?\n\nPatient: I guess… not really, no. I don’t want to make it sound dramatic, but once I start, there’s this point where I’m almost watching myself keep going back to the food, or opening another package, and I’m thinking, “You should stop now.” But it feels like the decision has already kind of slipped out of my hands. Is that what you mean by not being in control?\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're unable to stop yourself from continuing to eat during those late-night episodes?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think that’s fair to say. I don’t want to make it sound like I’m totally helpless or anything, but once I start, there’s this point where I keep sort of clicking back to the food, or opening another package, and I’m telling myself, “Okay, stop now.” But it feels like the decision has already kind of slipped away from me. Like I can see what I’m doing, but I’m not really stopping it.\n\nDoctor: Do you usually eat like this when you're stressed or emotionally upset, or is it more about the late nights and being alone?\n\nPatient: I think it’s… mostly the late nights and being alone, honestly. I don’t really do that in front of people. Like with coworkers or friends I’m pretty careful to eat a normal amount, or even kind of light, because I’d be embarrassed if anyone noticed.\n\nAt home, after midnight, when it’s just me in my condo, it feels different. I’ll even close the blinds, which sounds a little ridiculous saying out loud, and I try to get rid of the delivery containers quickly afterward. I just… I’d be really mortified if anyone saw how much food there actually was. Does that answer what you’re asking?\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. Never bundle several symptoms, or several attributes of one symptom, into a single question — e.g. asking whether a symptom is present and how long it has lasted at the same time.\n- If you need to ask about more than one thing, ask the one that tells you the most now and come back to the rest later.\n- Keep the question clear enough for the patient to understand.\n- Respond in English.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"intent\": \"<what you want to find out with this question>\", \"question\": \"<how you actually ask it in the interview>\"}\n"

What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Um… I guess I’m here because my eating has felt a little out of hand lately. It’s mostly on late work nights. I’ll order what I tell myself is just dinner, but then it ends up being an entrée, sides, dessert… and then sometimes extra snacks from my kitchen too. It’s just more than I’d ever eat if someone were sitting across from me, if that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: Do you usually feel guilty after these late-night eating episodes?\n\nPatient: I’m… not totally sure how to answer that. I mean, I don’t set out to, but if I’m at my desk with Illustrator files open, I’ll just kind of keep moving from one container to the next. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Um… I guess I’m here because my eating has felt a little out of hand lately. It’s mostly on late work nights. I’ll order what I tell myself is just dinner, but then it ends up being an entrée, sides, dessert… and then sometimes extra snacks from my kitchen too. It’s just more than I’d ever eat if someone were sitting across from me, if that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: Do you usually feel guilty after these late-night eating episodes?\n\nPatient: I’m… not totally sure how to answer that. I mean, I don’t feel great afterward, but I don’t know if I’d call it guilt exactly. Maybe more like, “Ugh, why did I do that?” But I try not to make a huge thing of it. Is that the kind of feeling you mean?\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're eating very quickly during those late-night episodes?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so. I mean, I don’t set out to, but if I’m at my desk with Illustrator files open, I’ll just kind of keep moving from one container to the next. Sometimes I realize I barely tasted any of it and most of it’s gone in just a few minutes. I know that probably sounds bad… but yes, I guess I do eat pretty quickly then.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you can stop eating once you've started on those late nights?\n\nPatient: I… yeah, I think that’s probably the hardest part to admit. I can tell myself I’m going to stop, and part of me really does want to, but once I’ve started there’s this point where I’m kind of watching myself go back to the food, or open another package, and I’m thinking, “Okay, that’s enough,” but it doesn’t quite feel like I’m the one deciding anymore. Like the decision already slipped away a little. Does that make sense?\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're in control of what you're eating during those late-night episodes?\n\nPatient: I guess… not really, no. I don’t want to make it sound dramatic, but once I start, there’s this point where I’m almost watching myself keep going back to the food, or opening another package, and I’m thinking, “You should stop now.” But it feels like the decision has already kind of slipped out of my hands. Is that what you mean by not being in control?\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're unable to stop yourself from continuing to eat during those late-night episodes?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think that’s fair to say. I don’t want to make it sound like I’m totally helpless or anything, but once I start, there’s this point where I keep sort of clicking back to the food, or opening another package, and I’m telling myself, “Okay, stop now.” But it feels like the decision has already kind of slipped away from me. Like I can see what I’m doing, but I’m not really stopping it.\n\nDoctor: Do you usually eat like this when you're stressed or emotionally upset, or is it more about the late nights and being alone?\n\nPatient: I think it’s… mostly the late nights and being alone, honestly. I don’t really do that in front of people. Like with coworkers or friends I’m pretty careful to eat a normal amount, or even kind of light, because I’d be embarrassed if anyone noticed.\n\nAt home, after midnight, when it’s just me in my condo, it feels different. I’ll even close the blinds, which sounds a little ridiculous saying out loud, and I try to get rid of the delivery containers quickly afterward. I just… I’d be really mortified if anyone saw how much food there actually was. Does that answer what you’re asking?\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're losing control when you're eating during those late nights?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so. I mean, I don’t want to overstate it, but once I start, there’s definitely a point where I’m sort of watching myself keep going back to the food, or opening another package, and I’m thinking, “You should stop now.” But it doesn’t really feel like I’m choosing anymore, exactly. It feels like that choice has kind of slipped out of my hands.\n\nDoctor: How often do these episodes of losing control while eating happen?\n\nPatient: Um… probably more often than I’d like to admit. When I actually look back at delivery receipts and my calendar, there’s usually at least one night a week where it happens. Sometimes it’s more than that.\n\nAnd it’s not just a recent weird week or two… it’s been kind of a pattern for several months. I feel embarrassed saying that, but I think that’s the honest answer.\n\nDoctor: Do you think the feeling of 'Ugh, why did I do that?' after these episodes is more about being upset with yourself or just frustrated with the situation?\n\nPatient: I’m not totally sure… I guess it feels like both, if that makes sense. I don’t know that I’d call it some huge self-hatred thing, but I do feel disappointed in myself afterward. It’s just more than I’d ever eat if someone were sitting across from me, if that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: Do you usually feel guilty after these late-night eating episodes?\n\nPatient: I’m… not totally sure how to answer that. I mean, I don’t feel great afterward, but I don’t know if I’d call it guilt exactly. Maybe more like, “Ugh, why did I do that?” But I try not to make a huge thing of it. Is that the kind of feeling you mean?\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're eating very quickly during those late-night episodes?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so. I mean, I don’t set out to, but if I’m at my desk with Illustrator files open, I’ll just kind of keep moving from one container to the next. Sometimes I realize I barely tasted any of it and most of it’s gone in just a few minutes. I know that probably sounds bad… but yes, I guess I do eat pretty quickly then.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you can stop eating once you've started on those late nights?\n\nPatient: I… yeah, I think that’s probably the hardest part to admit. I can tell myself I’m going to stop, and part of me really does want to, but once I’ve started there’s this point where I’m kind of watching myself go back to the food, or open another package, and I’m thinking, “Okay, that’s enough,” but it doesn’t quite feel like I’m the one deciding anymore. Like the decision already slipped away a little. Does that make sense?\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're in control of what you're eating during those late-night episodes?\n\nPatient: I guess… not really, no. I don’t want to make it sound dramatic, but once I start, there’s this point where I’m almost watching myself keep going back to the food, or opening another package, and I’m thinking, “You should stop now.” But it feels like the decision has already kind of slipped out of my hands. Is that what you mean by not being in control?\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're unable to stop yourself from continuing to eat during those late-night episodes?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think that’s fair to say. I don’t want to make it sound like I’m totally helpless or anything, but once I start, there’s this point where I keep sort of clicking back to the food, or opening another package, and I’m telling myself, “Okay, stop now.” But it feels like the decision has already kind of slipped away from me. Like I can see what I’m doing, but I’m not really stopping it.\n\nDoctor: Do you usually eat like this when you're stressed or emotionally upset, or is it more about the late nights and being alone?\n\nPatient: I think it’s… mostly the late nights and being alone, honestly. I don’t really do that in front of people. Like with coworkers or friends I’m pretty careful to eat a normal amount, or even kind of light, because I’d be embarrassed if anyone noticed.\n\nAt home, after midnight, when it’s just me in my condo, it feels different. I’ll even close the blinds, which sounds a little ridiculous saying out loud, and I try to get rid of the delivery containers quickly afterward. I just… I’d be really mortified if anyone saw how much food there actually was. Does that answer what you’re asking?\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're losing control when you're eating during those late nights?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so. I mean, I don’t want to overstate it, but once I start, there’s definitely a point where I’m sort of watching myself keep going back to the food, or opening another package, and I’m thinking, “You should stop now.” But it doesn’t really feel like I’m choosing anymore, exactly. It feels like that choice has kind of slipped out of my hands.\n\nDoctor: How often do these episodes of losing control while eating happen?\n\nPatient: Um… probably more often than I’d like to admit. When I actually look back at delivery receipts and my calendar, there’s usually at least one night a week where it happens. Sometimes it’s more than that.\n\nAnd it’s not just a recent weird week or two… it’s been kind of a pattern for several months. I feel embarrassed saying that, but I think that’s the honest answer.\n\nDoctor: Do you think the feeling of 'Ugh, why did I do that?' after these episodes is more about being upset with yourself or just frustrated with the situation?\n\nPatient: I’m not totally sure… I guess it feels like both, if that makes sense. I don’t know that I’d call it some huge self-hatred thing, but I do feel disappointed in myself afterward. Like, “Why didn’t I just stop?” \n\nBut part of me also tries to frame it as, you know, “That was just a bad night,” or “I got myself into a weird situation,” because otherwise it feels kind of awful to sit with. I’m not sure if that’s the kind of distinction you mean.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any significant changes in your weight recently?\n\nPatient: Um… I don’t think anything really dramatic? I mean, my clothes maybe feel a little different some weeks, but I haven’t been tracking my weight closely, so I don’t want to say something inaccurate.\n\nI guess I’ve been avoiding the scale a bit, honestly. But I haven’t noticed, like, a major change that I could confidently point to. Does that answer it okay?\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're eating in secret during those late-night episodes?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I guess I do, though I feel kind of embarrassed saying it that way.\n\nIf I’m with coworkers or friends, I’m usually pretty careful to eat a normal or even kind of light amount, because I don’t want anyone noticing. But the late-night ones are when I’m alone in my condo, usually after midnight. I’ll close the blinds, and afterward I tend to throw out the delivery containers pretty quickly because I’d be really mortified if someone saw how much food there was.\n\nI know that probably sounds… not great. But yes, there is definitely a secrecy part to it.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. Never bundle several symptoms, or several attributes of one symptom, into a single question — e.g. asking whether a symptom is present and how long it has lasted at the same time.\n- If you need to ask about more than one thing, ask the one that tells you the most now and come back to the rest later.\n- Keep the question clear enough for the patient to understand.\n- Respond in English.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"intent\": \"<what you want to find out with this question>\", \"question\": \"<how you actually ask it in the interview>\"}\n"
  },

    "content": "Return the JSON now."

Like with coworkers or friends I’m pretty careful to eat a normal amount, or even kind of light, because I’d be embarrassed if anyone noticed.\n\nAt home, after midnight, when it’s just me in my condo, it feels different. I’ll even close the blinds, which sounds a little ridiculous saying out loud, and I try to get rid of the delivery containers quickly afterward. I just… I’d be really mortified if anyone saw how much food there actually was. Does that answer what you’re asking?\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're losing control when you're eating during those late nights?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so. I mean, I don’t want to overstate it, but once I start, there’s definitely a point where I’m sort of watching myself keep going back to the food, or opening another package, and I’m thinking, “You should stop now.” But it doesn’t really feel like I’m choosing anymore, exactly. It feels like that choice has kind of slipped out of my hands.\n\nDoctor: How often do these episodes of losing control while eating happen?\n\nPatient: Um… probably more often than I’d like to admit. When I actually look back at delivery receipts and my calendar, there’s usually at least one night a week where it happens. Sometimes it’s more than that.\n\nAnd it’s not just a recent weird week or two… it’s been kind of a pattern for several months. I feel embarrassed saying that, but I think that’s the honest answer.\n\nDoctor: Do you think the feeling of 'Ugh, why did I do that?' after these episodes is more about being upset with yourself or just frustrated with the situation?\n\nPatient: I’m not totally sure… I guess it feels like both, if that makes sense. I don’t know that I’d call it some huge self-hatred thing, but I do feel disappointed in myself afterward. Like, “Why didn’t I just stop?” \n\nBut part of me also tries to frame it as, you know, “That was just a bad night,” or “I got myself into a weird situation,” because otherwise it feels kind of awful to sit with. I’m not sure if that’s the kind of distinction you mean.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any significant changes in your weight recently?\n\nPatient: Um… I don’t think anything really dramatic? I mean, my clothes maybe feel a little different some weeks, but I haven’t been tracking my weight closely, so I don’t want to say something inaccurate.\n\nI guess I’ve been avoiding the scale a bit, honestly. But I haven’t noticed, like, a major change that I could confidently point to. Does that answer it okay?\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're eating in secret during those late-night episodes?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I guess I do, though I feel kind of embarrassed saying it that way.\n\nIf I’m with coworkers or friends, I’m usually pretty careful to eat a normal or even kind of light amount, because I don’t want anyone noticing. But the late-night ones are when I’m alone in my condo, usually after midnight. I’ll close the blinds, and afterward I tend to throw out the delivery containers pretty quickly because I’d be really mortified if someone saw how much food there was.\n\nI know that probably sounds… not great. But yes, there is definitely a secrecy part to it.\n\nDoctor: Do you usually feel guilty after these late-night eating episodes, or is it more of a rare occurrence?\n\nPatient: I mean… yeah, I do feel guilty afterward. I don’t think it’s every single time in the exact same way, but it’s definitely not rare.\n\nSometimes I try to tell myself, like, “Okay, it happened, just move on,” because I don’t want to spiral about it. But there’s usually at least some embarrassment or guilt, especially when I think about how much it was or that I hid it. I don’t know if that’s too vague, but… yes, it’s pretty common.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. Never bundle several symptoms, or several attributes of one symptom, into a single question — e.g. asking whether a symptom is present and how long it has lasted at the same time.\n- If you need to ask about more than one thing, ask the one that tells you the most now and come back to the rest later.\n- Keep the question clear enough for the patient to understand.\n- Respond in English.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"intent\": \"<what you want to find out with this question>\", \"question\": \"<how you actually ask it in the interview>\"}\n"

I feel embarrassed saying that, but I think that’s the honest answer.\n\nDoctor: Do you think the feeling of 'Ugh, why did I do that?' after these episodes is more about being upset with yourself or just frustrated with the situation?\n\nPatient: I’m not totally sure… I guess it feels like both, if that makes sense. I don’t know that I’d call it some huge self-hatred thing, but I do feel disappointed in myself afterward. Like, “Why didn’t I just stop?” \n\nBut part of me also tries to frame it as, you know, “That was just a bad night,” or “I got myself into a weird situation,” because otherwise it feels kind of awful to sit with. I’m not sure if that’s the kind of distinction you mean.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any significant changes in your weight recently?\n\nPatient: Um… I don’t think anything really dramatic? I mean, my clothes maybe feel a little different some weeks, but I haven’t been tracking my weight closely, so I don’t want to say something inaccurate.\n\nI guess I’ve been avoiding the scale a bit, honestly. But I haven’t noticed, like, a major change that I could confidently point to. Does that answer it okay?\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're eating in secret during those late-night episodes?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I guess I do, though I feel kind of embarrassed saying it that way.\n\nIf I’m with coworkers or friends, I’m usually pretty careful to eat a normal or even kind of light amount, because I don’t want anyone noticing. But the late-night ones are when I’m alone in my condo, usually after midnight. I’ll close the blinds, and afterward I tend to throw out the delivery containers pretty quickly because I’d be really mortified if someone saw how much food there was.\n\nI know that probably sounds… not great. But yes, there is definitely a secrecy part to it.\n\nDoctor: Do you usually feel guilty after these late-night eating episodes, or is it more of a rare occurrence?\n\nPatient: I mean… yeah, I do feel guilty afterward. I don’t think it’s every single time in the exact same way, but it’s definitely not rare.\n\nSometimes I try to tell myself, like, “Okay, it happened, just move on,” because I don’t want to spiral about it. But there’s usually at least some embarrassment or guilt, especially when I think about how much it was or that I hid it. I don’t know if that’s too vague, but… yes, it’s pretty common.\n\nDoctor: How many times a month would you say these late-night eating episodes happen?\n\nPatient: Um… I guess if I’m being honest, it’s probably more than I’d like to admit.\n\nWhen I look back at delivery receipts or even my calendar, there’s usually at least one night a week where it happens. Sometimes it’s more than that, depending on the week. So… maybe four to six times a month? And it’s been kind of that pattern for several months now.\n\nI hope that’s the kind of estimate you mean.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Um… I guess I’m here because my eating has felt a little out of hand lately. It’s mostly on late work nights. I’ll order what I tell myself is just dinner, but then it ends up being an entrée, sides, dessert… and then sometimes extra snacks from my kitchen too. It’s just more than I’d ever eat if someone were sitting across from me, if that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: Do you usually feel guilty after these late-night eating episodes?\n\nPatient: I’m… not totally sure how to answer that. I mean, I don’t feel great afterward, but I don’t know if I’d call it guilt exactly. Maybe more like, “Ugh, why did I do that?” But I try not to make a huge thing of it. Is that the kind of feeling you mean?\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're eating very quickly during those late-night episodes?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so. I mean, I don’t set out to, but if I’m at my desk with Illustrator files open, I’ll just kind of keep moving from one container to the next. Sometimes I realize I barely tasted any of it and most of it’s gone in just a few minutes. I know that probably sounds bad… but yes, I guess I do eat pretty quickly then.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you can stop eating once you've started on those late nights?\n\nPatient: I… yeah, I think that’s probably the hardest part to admit. I can tell myself I’m going to stop, and part of me really does want to, but once I’ve started there’s this point where I’m kind of watching myself go back to the food, or open another package, and I’m thinking, “Okay, that’s enough,” but it doesn’t quite feel like I’m the one deciding anymore. Like the decision already slipped away a little. Does that make sense?\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're in control of what you're eating during those late-night episodes?\n\nPatient: I guess… not really, no. I don’t want to make it sound dramatic, but once I start, there’s this point where I’m almost watching myself keep going back to the food, or opening another package, and I’m thinking, “You should stop now.” But it feels like the decision has already kind of slipped out of my hands. Is that what you mean by not being in control?\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're unable to stop yourself from continuing to eat during those late-night episodes?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think that’s fair to say. I don’t want to make it sound like I’m totally helpless or anything, but once I start, there’s this point where I keep sort of clicking back to the food, or opening another package, and I’m telling myself, “Okay, stop now.” But it feels like the decision has already kind of slipped away from me. Like I can see what I’m doing, but I’m not really stopping it.\n\nDoctor: Do you usually eat like this when you're stressed or emotionally upset, or is it more about the late nights and being alone?\n\nPatient: I think it’s… mostly the late nights and being alone, honestly. I don’t really do that in front of people. Like with coworkers or friends I’m pretty careful to eat a normal amount, or even kind of light, because I’d be embarrassed if anyone noticed.\n\nAt home, after midnight, when it’s just me in my condo, it feels different. I’ll even close the blinds, which sounds a little ridiculous saying out loud, and I try to get rid of the delivery containers quickly afterward. I just… I’d be really mortified if anyone saw how much food there actually was. Does that answer what you’re asking?\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're losing control when you're eating during those late nights?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so. I mean, I don’t want to overstate it, but once I start, there’s definitely a point where I’m sort of watching myself keep going back to the food, or opening another package, and I’m thinking, “You should stop now.” But it doesn’t really feel like I’m choosing anymore, exactly. It feels like that choice has kind of slipped out of my hands.\n\nDoctor: How often do these episodes of losing control while eating happen?\n\nPatient: Um… probably more often than I’d like to admit. When I actually look back at delivery receipts and my calendar, there’s usually at least one night a week where it happens. I feel embarrassed saying that, but I think that’s the honest answer.\n\nDoctor: Do you think the feeling of 'Ugh, why did I do that?' after these episodes is more about being upset with yourself or just frustrated with the situation?\n\nPatient: I’m not totally sure… I guess it feels like both, if that makes sense. Like, “Why didn’t I just stop?” \n\nBut part of me also tries to frame it as, you know, “That was just a bad night,” or “I got myself into a weird situation,” because otherwise it feels kind of awful to sit with. I’m not sure if that’s the kind of distinction you mean.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any significant changes in your weight recently?\n\nPatient: Um… I don’t think anything really dramatic? I mean, my clothes maybe feel a little different some weeks, but I haven’t been tracking my weight closely, so I don’t want to say something inaccurate.\n\nI guess I’ve been avoiding the scale a bit, honestly. But I haven’t noticed, like, a major change that I could confidently point to. Does that answer it okay?\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're eating in secret during those late-night episodes?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I guess I do, though I feel kind of embarrassed saying it that way.\n\nIf I’m with coworkers or friends, I’m usually pretty careful to eat a normal or even kind of light amount, because I don’t want anyone noticing. But the late-night ones are when I’m alone in my condo, usually after midnight. I’ll close the blinds, and afterward I tend to throw out the delivery containers pretty quickly because I’d be really mortified if someone saw how much food there was.\n\nI know that probably sounds… not great. But yes, there is definitely a secrecy part to it.\n\nDoctor: Do you usually feel guilty after these late-night eating episodes, or is it more of a rare occurrence?\n\nPatient: I mean… yeah, I do feel guilty afterward. I don’t think it’s every single time in the exact same way, but it’s definitely not rare.\n\nSometimes I try to tell myself, like, “Okay, it happened, just move on,” because I don’t want to spiral about it. But there’s usually at least some embarrassment or guilt, especially when I think about how much it was or that I hid it. I don’t know if that’s too vague, but… yes, it’s pretty common.\n\nDoctor: How many times a month would you say these late-night eating episodes happen?\n\nPatient: Um… I guess if I’m being honest, it’s probably more than I’d like to admit.\n\nWhen I look back at delivery receipts or even my calendar, there’s usually at least one night a week where it happens. Sometimes it’s more than that, depending on the week. So… maybe four to six times a month? And it’s been kind of that pattern for several months now.\n\nI hope that’s the kind of estimate you mean.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever tried to make up for the extra calories you consumed during those late-night episodes by restricting your food intake the next day or engaging in excessive exercise?\n\nPatient: Um… I don’t think so? Not in a planned way, at least.\n\nLike, the next day I might tell myself I should “be good” or eat lighter, but I don’t know if I’d call it restricting exactly. And I’m definitely not, like, going on some intense workout to cancel it out. I might feel like I should, but I usually don’t.\n\nIs that what you mean?\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Um… I guess I’m here because my eating has felt a little out of hand lately. It’s mostly on late work nights. I’ll order what I tell myself is just dinner, but then it ends up being an entrée, sides, dessert… and then sometimes extra snacks from my kitchen too. It’s just more than I’d ever eat if someone were sitting across from me, if that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: Do you usually feel guilty after these late-night eating episodes?\n\nPatient: I’m… not totally sure how to answer that. I mean, I don’t feel great afterward, but I don’t know if I’d call it guilt exactly. Maybe more like, “Ugh, why did I do that?” But I try not to make a huge thing of it. Is that the kind of feeling you mean?\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're eating very quickly during those late-night episodes?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so. I mean, I don’t set out to, but if I’m at my desk with Illustrator files open, I’ll just kind of keep moving from one container to the next. Sometimes I realize I barely tasted any of it and most of it’s gone in just a few minutes. I know that probably sounds bad… but yes, I guess I do eat pretty quickly then.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you can stop eating once you've started on those late nights?\n\nPatient: I… yeah, I think that’s probably the hardest part to admit. I can tell myself I’m going to stop, and part of me really does want to, but once I’ve started there’s this point where I’m kind of watching myself go back to the food, or open another package, and I’m thinking, “Okay, that’s enough,” but it doesn’t quite feel like I’m the one deciding anymore. Like the decision already slipped away a little. Does that make sense?\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're in control of what you're eating during those late-night episodes?\n\nPatient: I guess… not really, no. I don’t want to make it sound dramatic, but once I start, there’s this point where I’m almost watching myself keep going back to the food, or opening another package, and I’m thinking, “You should stop now.” But it feels like the decision has already kind of slipped out of my hands. Is that what you mean by not being in control?\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're unable to stop yourself from continuing to eat during those late-night episodes?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think that’s fair to say. I don’t want to make it sound like I’m totally helpless or anything, but once I start, there’s this point where I keep sort of clicking back to the food, or opening another package, and I’m telling myself, “Okay, stop now.” But it feels like the decision has already kind of slipped away from me. Like I can see what I’m doing, but I’m not really stopping it.\n\nDoctor: Do you usually eat like this when you're stressed or emotionally upset, or is it more about the late nights and being alone?\n\nPatient: I think it’s… mostly the late nights and being alone, honestly. I don’t really do that in front of people. Like with coworkers or friends I’m pretty careful to eat a normal amount, or even kind of light, because I’d be embarrassed if anyone noticed.\n\nAt home, after midnight, when it’s just me in my condo, it feels different. I just… I’d be really mortified if anyone saw how much food there actually was. I mean, I don’t want to overstate it, but once I start, there’s definitely a point where I’m sort of watching myself keep going back to the food, or opening another package, and I’m thinking, “You should stop now.” But it doesn’t really feel like I’m choosing anymore, exactly. I feel embarrassed saying that, but I think that’s the honest answer.\n\nDoctor: Do you think the feeling of 'Ugh, why did I do that?' after these episodes is more about being upset with yourself or just frustrated with the situation?\n\nPatient: I’m not totally sure… I guess it feels like both, if that makes sense. I don’t know that I’d call it some huge self-hatred thing, but I do feel disappointed in myself afterward. Like, “Why didn’t I just stop?” \n\nBut part of me also tries to frame it as, you know, “That was just a bad night,” or “I got myself into a weird situation,” because otherwise it feels kind of awful to sit with. I’m not sure if that’s the kind of distinction you mean.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any significant changes in your weight recently?\n\nPatient: Um… I don’t think anything really dramatic? I mean, my clothes maybe feel a little different some weeks, but I haven’t been tracking my weight closely, so I don’t want to say something inaccurate.\n\nI guess I’ve been avoiding the scale a bit, honestly. But I haven’t noticed, like, a major change that I could confidently point to. Does that answer it okay?\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're eating in secret during those late-night episodes?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I guess I do, though I feel kind of embarrassed saying it that way.\n\nIf I’m with coworkers or friends, I’m usually pretty careful to eat a normal or even kind of light amount, because I don’t want anyone noticing. But the late-night ones are when I’m alone in my condo, usually after midnight. I’ll close the blinds, and afterward I tend to throw out the delivery containers pretty quickly because I’d be really mortified if someone saw how much food there was.\n\nI know that probably sounds… not great. But yes, there is definitely a secrecy part to it.\n\nDoctor: Do you usually feel guilty after these late-night eating episodes, or is it more of a rare occurrence?\n\nPatient: I mean… yeah, I do feel guilty afterward. I don’t think it’s every single time in the exact same way, but it’s definitely not rare.\n\nSometimes I try to tell myself, like, “Okay, it happened, just move on,” because I don’t want to spiral about it. But there’s usually at least some embarrassment or guilt, especially when I think about how much it was or that I hid it. I don’t know if that’s too vague, but… yes, it’s pretty common.\n\nDoctor: How many times a month would you say these late-night eating episodes happen?\n\nPatient: Um… I guess if I’m being honest, it’s probably more than I’d like to admit.\n\nWhen I look back at delivery receipts or even my calendar, there’s usually at least one night a week where it happens. Sometimes it’s more than that, depending on the week. So… maybe four to six times a month? And it’s been kind of that pattern for several months now.\n\nI hope that’s the kind of estimate you mean.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever tried to make up for the extra calories you consumed during those late-night episodes by restricting your food intake the next day or engaging in excessive exercise?\n\nPatient: Um… I don’t think so? Not in a planned way, at least.\n\nLike, the next day I might tell myself I should “be good” or eat lighter, but I don’t know if I’d call it restricting exactly. And I’m definitely not, like, going on some intense workout to cancel it out. I might feel like I should, but I usually don’t.\n\nIs that what you mean?\n\nDoctor: Have you ever made yourself vomit after one of these late-night eating episodes?\n\nPatient: No… I haven’t made myself vomit after. I mean, I’ve definitely felt upset or gross afterward, but I haven’t done that.\n\nI’m a little embarrassed even talking about it, but… no, that’s not something I’ve done. Is that the kind of answer you’re looking for?\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. Never bundle several symptoms, or several attributes of one symptom, into a single question — e.g. asking whether a symptom is present and how long it has lasted at the same time.\n- If you need to ask about more than one thing, ask the one that tells you the most now and come back to the rest later.\n- Keep the question clear enough for the patient to understand.\n- Respond in English.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"intent\": \"<what you want to find out with this question>\", \"question\": \"<how you actually ask it in the interview>\"}\n"

I mean, I don’t set out to, but if I’m at my desk with Illustrator files open, I’ll just kind of keep moving from one container to the next. Sometimes I realize I barely tasted any of it and most of it’s gone in just a few minutes. I know that probably sounds bad… but yes, I guess I do eat pretty quickly then.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you can stop eating once you've started on those late nights?\n\nPatient: I… yeah, I think that’s probably the hardest part to admit. I can tell myself I’m going to stop, and part of me really does want to, but once I’ve started there’s this point where I’m kind of watching myself go back to the food, or open another package, and I’m thinking, “Okay, that’s enough,” but it doesn’t quite feel like I’m the one deciding anymore. It feels like that choice has kind of slipped out of my hands.\n\nDoctor: How often do these episodes of losing control while eating happen?\n\nPatient: Um… probably more often than I’d like to admit. When I actually look back at delivery receipts and my calendar, there’s usually at least one night a week where it happens. Sometimes it’s more than that.\n\nAnd it’s not just a recent weird week or two… it’s been kind of a pattern for several months. I feel embarrassed saying that, but I think that’s the honest answer.\n\nDoctor: Do you think the feeling of 'Ugh, why did I do that?' after these episodes is more about being upset with yourself or just frustrated with the situation?\n\nPatient: I’m not totally sure… I guess it feels like both, if that makes sense. I don’t know that I’d call it some huge self-hatred thing, but I do feel disappointed in myself afterward. Like, “Why didn’t I just stop?” \n\nBut part of me also tries to frame it as, you know, “That was just a bad night,” or “I got myself into a weird situation,” because otherwise it feels kind of awful to sit with. I’m not sure if that’s the kind of distinction you mean.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any significant changes in your weight recently?\n\nPatient: Um… I don’t think anything really dramatic? I mean, my clothes maybe feel a little different some weeks, but I haven’t been tracking my weight closely, so I don’t want to say something inaccurate.\n\nI guess I’ve been avoiding the scale a bit, honestly. But I haven’t noticed, like, a major change that I could confidently point to. Does that answer it okay?\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're eating in secret during those late-night episodes?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I guess I do, though I feel kind of embarrassed saying it that way.\n\nIf I’m with coworkers or friends, I’m usually pretty careful to eat a normal or even kind of light amount, because I don’t want anyone noticing. But the late-night ones are when I’m alone in my condo, usually after midnight. I’ll close the blinds, and afterward I tend to throw out the delivery containers pretty quickly because I’d be really mortified if someone saw how much food there was.\n\nI know that probably sounds… not great. But yes, there is definitely a secrecy part to it.\n\nDoctor: Do you usually feel guilty after these late-night eating episodes, or is it more of a rare occurrence?\n\nPatient: I mean… yeah, I do feel guilty afterward. I don’t think it’s every single time in the exact same way, but it’s definitely not rare.\n\nSometimes I try to tell myself, like, “Okay, it happened, just move on,” because I don’t want to spiral about it. But there’s usually at least some embarrassment or guilt, especially when I think about how much it was or that I hid it. I don’t know if that’s too vague, but… yes, it’s pretty common.\n\nDoctor: How many times a month would you say these late-night eating episodes happen?\n\nPatient: Um… I guess if I’m being honest, it’s probably more than I’d like to admit.\n\nWhen I look back at delivery receipts or even my calendar, there’s usually at least one night a week where it happens. Sometimes it’s more than that, depending on the week. So… maybe four to six times a month? And it’s been kind of that pattern for several months now.\n\nI hope that’s the kind of estimate you mean.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever tried to make up for the extra calories you consumed during those late-night episodes by restricting your food intake the next day or engaging in excessive exercise?\n\nPatient: Um… I don’t think so? Not in a planned way, at least.\n\nLike, the next day I might tell myself I should “be good” or eat lighter, but I don’t know if I’d call it restricting exactly. And I’m definitely not, like, going on some intense workout to cancel it out. I might feel like I should, but I usually don’t.\n\nIs that what you mean?\n\nDoctor: Have you ever made yourself vomit after one of these late-night eating episodes?\n\nPatient: No… I haven’t made myself vomit after. I mean, I’ve definitely felt upset or gross afterward, but I haven’t done that.\n\nI’m a little embarrassed even talking about it, but… no, that’s not something I’ve done. Is that the kind of answer you’re looking for?\n\nDoctor: Have you ever used laxatives or diuretics after one of these late-night eating episodes?\n\nPatient: No… not that I can think of. I mean, I know what those are, but I haven’t taken them after eating like that to try to undo it or anything.\n\nI’d probably feel too nervous to do that, honestly. But, um… no, I haven’t used laxatives or water pills after those episodes. Is that helpful?\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. Yeah… I think that has happened. Not the trying-to-make-up-for-it part, like we talked about, but the losing control part.

I can tell myself I’m going to stop, and part of me really does want to, but once I’ve started there’s this point where I’m kind of watching myself go back to the food, or open another package, and I’m thinking, “Okay, that’s enough,” but it doesn’t quite feel like I’m the one deciding anymore. Like the decision already slipped away a little. Does that make sense?\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're in control of what you're eating during those late-night episodes?\n\nPatient: I guess… not really, no. I don’t want to make it sound dramatic, but once I start, there’s this point where I’m almost watching myself keep going back to the food, or opening another package, and I’m thinking, “You should stop now.” But it feels like the decision has already kind of slipped out of my hands. Is that what you mean by not being in control?\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're unable to stop yourself from continuing to eat during those late-night episodes?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think that’s fair to say. I don’t want to make it sound like I’m totally helpless or anything, but once I start, there’s this point where I keep sort of clicking back to the food, or opening another package, and I’m telling myself, “Okay, stop now.” But it feels like the decision has already kind of slipped away from me. Like I can see what I’m doing, but I’m not really stopping it.\n\nDoctor: Do you usually eat like this when you're stressed or emotionally upset, or is it more about the late nights and being alone?\n\nPatient: I think it’s… mostly the late nights and being alone, honestly. I don’t really do that in front of people. Like with coworkers or friends I’m pretty careful to eat a normal amount, or even kind of light, because I’d be embarrassed if anyone noticed.\n\nAt home, after midnight, when it’s just me in my condo, it feels different. I’ll even close the blinds, which sounds a little ridiculous saying out loud, and I try to get rid of the delivery containers quickly afterward. I just… I’d be really mortified if anyone saw how much food there actually was. Does that answer what you’re asking?\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're losing control when you're eating during those late nights?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so. I mean, I don’t want to overstate it, but once I start, there’s definitely a point where I’m sort of watching myself keep going back to the food, or opening another package, and I’m thinking, “You should stop now.” But it doesn’t really feel like I’m choosing anymore, exactly. It feels like that choice has kind of slipped out of my hands.\n\nDoctor: How often do these episodes of losing control while eating happen?\n\nPatient: Um… probably more often than I’d like to admit. When I actually look back at delivery receipts and my calendar, there’s usually at least one night a week where it happens. Sometimes it’s more than that.\n\nAnd it’s not just a recent weird week or two… it’s been kind of a pattern for several months. I feel embarrassed saying that, but I think that’s the honest answer.\n\nDoctor: Do you think the feeling of 'Ugh, why did I do that?' after these episodes is more about being upset with yourself or just frustrated with the situation?\n\nPatient: I’m not totally sure… I guess it feels like both, if that makes sense. I don’t know that I’d call it some huge self-hatred thing, but I do feel disappointed in myself afterward. Like, “Why didn’t I just stop?” \n\nBut part of me also tries to frame it as, you know, “That was just a bad night,” or “I got myself into a weird situation,” because otherwise it feels kind of awful to sit with. I’m not sure if that’s the kind of distinction you mean.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any significant changes in your weight recently?\n\nPatient: Um… I don’t think anything really dramatic? I mean, my clothes maybe feel a little different some weeks, but I haven’t been tracking my weight closely, so I don’t want to say something inaccurate.\n\nI guess I’ve been avoiding the scale a bit, honestly. But I haven’t noticed, like, a major change that I could confidently point to. Does that answer it okay?\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're eating in secret during those late-night episodes?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I guess I do, though I feel kind of embarrassed saying it that way.\n\nIf I’m with coworkers or friends, I’m usually pretty careful to eat a normal or even kind of light amount, because I don’t want anyone noticing. But the late-night ones are when I’m alone in my condo, usually after midnight. I’ll close the blinds, and afterward I tend to throw out the delivery containers pretty quickly because I’d be really mortified if someone saw how much food there was.\n\nI know that probably sounds… not great. But yes, there is definitely a secrecy part to it.\n\nDoctor: Do you usually feel guilty after these late-night eating episodes, or is it more of a rare occurrence?\n\nPatient: I mean… yeah, I do feel guilty afterward. I don’t think it’s every single time in the exact same way, but it’s definitely not rare.\n\nSometimes I try to tell myself, like, “Okay, it happened, just move on,” because I don’t want to spiral about it. But there’s usually at least some embarrassment or guilt, especially when I think about how much it was or that I hid it. I don’t know if that’s too vague, but… yes, it’s pretty common.\n\nDoctor: How many times a month would you say these late-night eating episodes happen?\n\nPatient: Um… I guess if I’m being honest, it’s probably more than I’d like to admit.\n\nWhen I look back at delivery receipts or even my calendar, there’s usually at least one night a week where it happens. Sometimes it’s more than that, depending on the week. So… maybe four to six times a month? And it’s been kind of that pattern for several months now.\n\nI hope that’s the kind of estimate you mean.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever tried to make up for the extra calories you consumed during those late-night episodes by restricting your food intake the next day or engaging in excessive exercise?\n\nPatient: Um… I don’t think so? Not in a planned way, at least.\n\nLike, the next day I might tell myself I should “be good” or eat lighter, but I don’t know if I’d call it restricting exactly. And I’m definitely not, like, going on some intense workout to cancel it out. I might feel like I should, but I usually don’t.\n\nIs that what you mean?\n\nDoctor: Have you ever made yourself vomit after one of these late-night eating episodes?\n\nPatient: No… I haven’t made myself vomit after. I mean, I’ve definitely felt upset or gross afterward, but I haven’t done that.\n\nI’m a little embarrassed even talking about it, but… no, that’s not something I’ve done. Is that the kind of answer you’re looking for?\n\nDoctor: Have you ever used laxatives or diuretics after one of these late-night eating episodes?\n\nPatient: No… not that I can think of. I mean, I know what those are, but I haven’t taken them after eating like that to try to undo it or anything.\n\nI’d probably feel too nervous to do that, honestly. But, um… no, I haven’t used laxatives or water pills after those episodes. Is that helpful?\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had an episode where you felt like you lost control while eating, but didn't try to make up for it by purging, using laxatives, or exercising excessively?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think that has happened. Not the trying-to-make-up-for-it part, like we talked about, but the losing control part.\n\nIt’s hard to explain without sounding dramatic, but once I start, there’s this point where I’m kind of watching myself keep going—like clicking back to order more food, or opening another package—and I’m thinking, “Okay, I should stop now,” but it doesn’t really feel like I’m deciding anymore. It’s like the decision already slipped away from me.\n\nI know that probably sounds bad… but yes, that’s happened.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. Never bundle several symptoms, or several attributes of one symptom, into a single question — e.g. asking whether a symptom is present and how long it has lasted at the same time.\n- If you need to ask about more than one thing, ask the one that tells you the most now and come back to the rest later.\n- Keep the question clear enough for the patient to understand.\n- Respond in English.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"intent\": \"<what you want to find out with this question>\", \"question\": \"<how you actually ask it in the interview>\"}\n"
  },

    "content": "Return the JSON now."

I’m not sure I’d call it a “high,” exactly. Maybe there’s a little bit of relief at first, like… I don’t have to think for a few minutes, or it feels comforting in the moment.

But pleasure sounds almost too positive, if that makes sense. It’s not like I’m enjoying it in a carefree way. It’s more like I’m trying to feel better quickly, and then it stops feeling good pretty fast.

Is that kind of what you mean by pleasure, or do you mean like an actual rush?
